follows from the sub-Gaussian tail in Proposition 2.6.6 of
Vershynin (2018)
.

Bounding Term
(B)
.

We define the change of variable
\mathbf{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{t}
and denote by
\mathbf{y}
the realization of
\mathbf{Y}
. Given
S>\min\{B/2,1\}
, applying the sub-Gaussian tail of
p_{\textrm{fac}}(\mathbf{f})
in (
19
), we obtain that

\displaystyle(B)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left\|\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}>S}\int_{\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathbf{f}\|_{2}>\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}}\phi(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y};\alpha_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{I})\cdot\frac{C_{1}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{k}{2}}}\|\mathbf{f}\|_{2}^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\|\mathbf{f}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right){\rm d}\mathbf{f}{\rm d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{C_{1}}{(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\left\|\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}>S}\int_{\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathbf{f}\|_{2}>\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{C_{2}\big\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}+C_{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\big\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\|\mathbf{f}\|_{2}^{2}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{\big\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbf{f}-\alpha_{t}(\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})^{-1}\mathbf{y}\right)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\Bigg){\rm d}\mathbf{f}{\rm d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{C_{1}}{(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\left\|\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}>S}\int_{\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathbf{f}\|_{2}>\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{C_{2}\big\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}+C_{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\big\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\|\mathbf{f}\|_{2}^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\left\|\mathbf{f}-\alpha_{t}(\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})^{-1}\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right){\rm d}\mathbf{f}{\rm d}\mathbf{y},

(B.40)

where
(i)
invokes the formula of
\phi(\mathbf{y};\alpha_{t}\mathbf{f},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t})
in (
12
) and completing the square for
\mathbf{f}
,
(ii)
follows from
\|\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}\|_{\textrm{op}}\geq C_{2}
.

Furthermore, applying
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{f}\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\alpha_{t}^{2}\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}+k
to (
B.40
), we deduce that

\displaystyle(B)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{C_{2}^{\frac{k}{2}}(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\left\|\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}>S}\left[\alpha_{t}^{2}\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{-1}+C_{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})^{-1}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}+k\right]\cdot\exp\Bigg(-\frac{C_{2}\big\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}+C_{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{y}\big\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\Bigg){\rm d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}2^{-\frac{k}{2}+2}kS^{k}}{C_{2}\Gamma(\frac{k}{2}+1)(\alpha_{t}^{2}+C_{2}(h_{t}+\sigma_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2}))}\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{t}^{2}+C_{2}(h_{t}+\sigma_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2}))C_{2}S^{2}}{2}\right),

(B.41)

where the last inequality is due to
\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}\|_{\textrm{op}}\geq h_{t}+\sigma_{d}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2}
,
\|(\alpha_{t}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}+C_{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\textrm{op}}\geq\sqrt{\alpha_{t}^{2}+C_{2}(h_{t}+\sigma_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2})}
and the sub-Gaussian tail in Proposition 2.6.6 of
Vershynin (2018)
and similar operator norm bounds in
(ii)
.

Combining the error bounds (
B.39
) and (
B.41
) for
(A)
and
(B)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\left(\mathbf{Z},t\right)\mathbbm{1}\{\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{2}>S\}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(P_{t}^{\rm fac}\right)}\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\frac{2^{-\frac{k}{2}+3}k(h_{t}+\sigma_{d}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2})^{\frac{k}{2}}S^{k}}{\Gamma(\frac{k}{2}+1)(\alpha_{t}^{2}+C_{2}(h_{t}+\sigma_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2}))}\exp\left(-\frac{(h_{t}+\sigma_{d}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{2})S^{2}}{8}\right)

(B.42)

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Given any
\epsilon>0
, by the upper bound of truncation error in (
B.42
), we can choose

S=c\left(\sqrt{\bigg(1+\sigma_{\max}^{2}\bigg)\bigg(k+\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg)}\right),

such that
\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\left(\mathbf{Z},t\right)\mathbbm{1}\{\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{2}>S\}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(P_{t}^{\rm fac}\right)}\leq\epsilon
. Here,
c
is an absolute constant.
\square

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose Assumption
2
holds. For any
\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}\left(\cdot,t\right)
and
\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}\left(\cdot,t\right)
, when
\rho
is sufficiently large, it holds that

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq\rho}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t}|\mathbf{R}_{0}=\mathbf{r}}\bigg[\left(K+\left\|\mathbf{R}_{t}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}\left(\mathbf{R}_{t},t\right)-\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}\left(\mathbf{R}_{t},t\right)\right\|_{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{R}_{t}\right\|_{2}>3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}\right\}\bigg]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\rho K^{2}h_{t}^{-2-\frac{d}{2}}\left(\frac{\rho}{d}\right)^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{\rho^{2}}{h_{t}}\right)\bigg).

(B.43)

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Denote
\mathbf{D}_{ti}=\operatorname{diag}\{1/(h_{t}+c_{i1}\alpha_{t}^{2}),\dots,1/(h_{t}+c_{i1}\alpha_{t}^{2})\}
for
i=1,2
. Applying the formula of
\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}
and
\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}
in (
18
), we calculate

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t}|\mathbf{R}_{0}=\mathbf{r}}\bigg[\left(K+\left\|\mathbf{R}_{t}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}\left(\mathbf{R}_{t},t\right)-\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}\left(\mathbf{R}_{t},t\right)\right\|_{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{R}_{t}\right\|_{2}>3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}\right\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\displaystyle\int\bigg(K+\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{2}\bigg)\bigg(\|(\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2})\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|\alpha_{t}(\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2})\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r}^{\prime},t)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\alpha_{t}\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2}(\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r}^{\prime},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{r}^{\prime},t))\|_{2}\bigg)\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}>3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}\right\}\phi(\mathbf{r}^{\prime};\alpha_{t}\mathbf{r},h_{t}\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\|_{2}>3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}}\frac{(K+\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{2})(K+\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2})}{h_{t}^{2}(2\pi h_{t})^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right){\rm d}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\bigg),

(B.44)

where
(i)
is due to Cauchy-Schwarz inequality;
(ii)
follows from the upper bounds (
15
) and (
18
) of
\{\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}},\mathbf{V}_{i},\mathbf{D}_{ti}\}_{i=1,2}
,
\alpha_{t}^{2}\leq 1
and

\phi(\mathbf{r}^{\prime};\alpha_{t}\mathbf{r},h_{t}\mathbf{I})\leq(2\pi h_{t})^{-\frac{d}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right).

Then, substituting the upper bound for the tail estimation in Proposition 2.6.6 of
Vershynin (2018)
into (
B.44
), we deduce that

(
B.44
)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{(K^{2}+K\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2})(2h_{t})^{-2-\frac{d}{2}}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{d-2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}\exp\left(-\frac{(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{2}}{4h_{t}}+\frac{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(2K+\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2})(2h_{t})^{-2-\frac{d}{2}}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{d-1}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}\exp\left(-\frac{(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{2}}{4h_{t}}+\frac{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(2h_{t})^{-2-\frac{d}{2}}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{d}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}\exp\left(-\frac{(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{2}}{4h_{t}}+\frac{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\left(K^{2}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}h_{t}^{-2-\frac{d}{2}}\left(\frac{\rho}{d}\right)^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{9\rho^{2}-2\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}}{4h_{t}}\right)\right).

(B.45)

Here, the last inequality holds since
\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)={\mathcal{O}}(\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{d}{2}}j)
and

\frac{2^{-\frac{d}{2}}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{6\rho\sqrt{d\log d}+d\log d}{4h_{t}}\right)}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\left(\frac{\rho}{d}\right)^{d}\bigg)

for a sufficiently large
\rho>\max\{B,d\}
.

Immediately, substituting
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq\rho
into (
B.45
), we obtain the desired result.

\square

Lemma B.4
.

For any given
\epsilon>0
,
\delta>0
, and
\rho={\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}^{2}\left(d+\log K+\log(n/\delta)\right)}\right)
defined in (
B.25
), the
\nu
-covering number of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm NN}
in (
18
) is

\displaystyle\log\mathfrak{N}(\nu,{\mathcal{S}}_{\textrm{NN}},\|\cdot\|_{2})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bigg(dk+T\tau(1+L_{s})^{k}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})\epsilon^{-(k+1)}\bigg(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+k\bigg)^{\frac{k+4}{2}}\bigg)\log\frac{T\tau dk}{t_{0}\nu\epsilon}\bigg).

(B.46)

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

{\mathcal{S}}_{\textrm{NN}}
consists of three components:

1.

A vector
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{d})\in[0,\sigma_{\max}]^{d}
and its induced matrix

\mathbf{D}_{t}=\operatorname{diag}\{1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{1}),1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{2}),\dots,1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{d})\}.

2.

A matrix
\mathbf{V}
with orthonormal columns.

3.

A ReLU network
\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
.

Denote
\mathbf{D}_{ti}=\operatorname{diag}\{1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{i1}),1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{i2}),\dots,1/(h_{t}+\alpha_{t}^{2}c_{id})\}
for
i=1,2
. Directly incorporating the sub-additive property of
L^{2}
norm and
\alpha_{t}^{2}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\|\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{1})\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}+\|(\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2})\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2}(\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t))\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2}(\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t))\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{V}_{2}(\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t2}\mathbf{r},t))\|_{2}+\|(\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2})\mathbf{r}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{V}_{2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t1}\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}+\gamma\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{D}_{t1}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma\|\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2},

(B.47)

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}_{i=1,2}
are orthogonal and
\{\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{i}}\}_{i=1,2}
is
\gamma
-Lipschitz.

To analyze the covering number of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\textrm{NN}}
, we consider

\|\mathbf{c}_{1}-\mathbf{c}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta_{c},\|\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{V}_{2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\delta_{V},\textrm{ and }\sup_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq 3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d},t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{1}}(\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}}(\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}\leq\delta_{f}.

(B.48)

Immediately, we can deduce that

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\mathbf{D}_{t1}-\mathbf{D}_{t2}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\frac{\delta_{c}}{t_{0}^{2}}.

(B.49)

Then, on the domain with radius
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq 3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}
and
t\in[t_{0},T]
, by substituting the upper bounds (
B.48
) and (
B.49
) into (
B.47
), we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq 3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d},t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\mathbf{r},t)-\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\mathbf{r},t)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta_{c}K}{t_{0}^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{V}K}{t_{0}}+\frac{\delta_{f}}{t_{0}}+\frac{\gamma\delta_{V}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})}{t_{0}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma\delta_{c}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})}{t_{0}^{3}}+\frac{\delta_{c}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})}{t_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\frac{\delta_{c}(\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})+t_{0}K+t_{0}(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d}))}{t_{0}^{3}}+\frac{\delta_{V}(\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})+t_{0}K)}{t_{0}^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{f}}{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\delta_{c}\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})+t_{0}\delta_{V}\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})+t_{0}^{2}\delta_{f}}{t_{0}^{3}}\right),

where
(i)
follows from rearranging terms, and
(ii)
holds by omitting higher-order terms on
\delta_{c}
,
\delta_{V}
, and
\delta_{f}
. For a hypercube
[0,\sigma_{\max}]^{d}
, the
\delta_{c}
-covering number is bounded by
\left(\frac{\sigma_{\max}}{\delta_{c}}\right)^{d}
. For a set of matrices
\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq 1\}
, its
\delta_{V}
-covering number is bounded by
\left(1+\frac{2\sqrt{k}}{\delta_{V}}\right)^{dk}
(a standard volume-ratio bound for matrices with a bounded operator norm; see Lemma 5.7 and Example 5.8 of
Wainwright (2019)
and Lemma 8 in
Chen, Li, and Zhao (2020)
). Following Lemma 5.3 in
Chen et al. (2022)
, which provides
\delta_{f}
-covering number bounds for ReLU network classes (see also Chapters 14 and 16 of
Anthony and Bartlett (2009)
), we take the upper bound
\left(\frac{2L^{2}M(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})\kappa^{L}M^{L+1}}{\delta_{f}}\right)^{J}
for the
\delta_{f}
-covering number of the function class (
15
). Therefore, with
\rho={\mathcal{O}}\big(\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}^{2}(d+\log K+\log(n/\delta))}\big)
, we have

\displaystyle\log\mathfrak{N}(\nu,{\mathcal{S}}_{\textrm{NN}},\|\cdot\|_{2})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\log\left(\frac{\sigma_{\max}\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})}{t_{0}^{3}\nu}\right)+dk\log\left(1+\frac{2\sqrt{k}\gamma(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})}{t_{0}^{2}\nu}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.+J\log\left(\frac{2L^{2}M(3\rho+\sqrt{d\log d})\kappa^{L}M^{L+1}}{t_{0}\nu}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\bigg(dk+T\tau(1+L_{s})^{k}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})\epsilon^{-(k+1)}\bigg(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+k\bigg)^{\frac{k+4}{2}}\bigg)\log\frac{T\tau dk}{t_{0}\nu\epsilon}\bigg),

where
(i)
follows from invoking the order of network parameters in (
22
) in Theorem
1
and omitting higher orders terms such as
\log\log d
and
\log\log k
.
\square

Appendix C
Omitted Proofs in Section
5

In this section, we provide the proof of Theorem
3
and the lemmas used in the proof.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

The proof contains two parts: the distribution estimation and the latent subspace recovery. For notational simplicity, let us denote

\epsilon:=\frac{1}{t_{0}}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{2k})d^{\frac{5}{2}}k^{\frac{k+10}{2}}n^{-\frac{2-2\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{4}n.

(C.1)

Part 1: Return Distribution Estimation.

First, we can decompose
\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})
into

\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})\leq\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t_{0}})+\operatorname{TV}(P_{t_{0}},\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})+\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}),

where
P_{\textrm{data}}
is the initial distribution of
\mathbf{R}
in (
8
),
\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}
and
\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}
are the marginal distribution of the estimated backward process
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}
in (
4
) initialized with
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
and
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim P_{T}
, respectively. Here,
\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t_{0}})
is the early-stopping error,
\operatorname{TV}(P_{t_{0}},\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})
captures the approximation error of the score estimation, and
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})
reflects the mixing error. We bound each term in Lemma
C.1
and the error bound (
C.28
) is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{TV}\left(P_{\textrm{data}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(dt_{0}L_{s}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{2})+\sqrt{\epsilon(T-t_{0})}+\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(P_{\textrm{data}}\|\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\right)}\exp(-T)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left((1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{2(k+5)}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\right)

where the last equality follows from invoking the order of
\epsilon
in (
C.1
),
t_{0}=n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}
, and
T={\mathcal{O}}(\log n)
and omitting the lower-order terms in
dt_{0}
. Hence the distribution estimation result in Theorem
3
is completed.

Part 2: Latent Subspace Recovery.
First, we generate
m={\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda_{\max}^{-2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})dn^{\frac{2(1-\delta(n))}{k+5}}\log n\right)
new samples via Algorithm
1
. By the error bound (
26
) in Lemma
2
, we obtain that, with probability
1-1/n
, it holds

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\right\|_{\textrm{op}}=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\Bigg(\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\Bigg).

Therefore, applying Weyl’s theorem to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
, we deduce that for any
i=1,2,\dots,d
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0})-\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})\right|=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\Bigg(\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\Bigg).

In addition, for any
i=1,2,\dots,k
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0})}{\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})}-1\right|=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\Bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}}{\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\Bigg).

Next, we analyze the SVD of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
. Recall that the top
k
-dimensional eigenspace of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
are denoted as
\mathbf{U}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
, respectively. For any
j=1,2,\dots,k
, define

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\cos\angle_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}):=\max_{\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\in\operatorname{Col}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}),\mathbf{u}\in\operatorname{Col}(\mathbf{U})}\frac{|\widehat{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{u}|}{\|\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}}:=|\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{j}|,

\displaystyle\textrm{ subject to }\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}=0\textrm{ and }\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{\ell}=0,\textrm{ for any }\ell=1,2,\dots,j-1,

where
\operatorname{Col}(\cdot)
represents the column space,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
. Applying Davis-Kahan-sin
(\theta)
Theorem of
Davis and Kahan (1970)
to
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
and
\mathbf{U}
, we have

\|\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U})\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\frac{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\|_{\rm{F}}}{\lambda_{k}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})-\lambda_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})}={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}}{{\textrm{\tt Eigen-gap}(k)}}\cdot n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\bigg),

(C.2)

where

\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}):=\Big(1-\cos^{2}\angle_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}),1-\cos^{2}\angle_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}),\dots,1-\cos^{2}\angle_{k}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U})\Big).

(C.3)

The inequality in (
C.2
) follows from the fact that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\textrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{k}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\textrm{op}}
for any
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
. By the property of SVD, we can find two orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{O}_{1},\mathbf{O}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{O}_{1}^{\top}\operatorname{diag}\left\{\cos\angle_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}),\cos\angle_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}),\dots,\cos\angle_{k}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U})\right\}\mathbf{O}_{2}.

Immediately, it holds that

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}=\mathbf{O}_{1}^{\top}\operatorname{diag}\left\{\cos^{2}(\angle_{1}),\dots,\cos^{2}(\angle_{k})\right\}\mathbf{O}_{1}.

(C.4)

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}+\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}-2\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{U}})+\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})-2\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{O}_{1}^{\top}\operatorname{diag}\left\{\cos^{2}(\angle_{1}),\dots,\cos^{2}(\angle_{k})\right\}\mathbf{O}_{1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}2k-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\cos^{2}(\angle_{i})=2\|\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U})\|_{\rm{F}}^{2},

(C.5)

where
(i)
invokes (
C.4
) and
(ii)
holds due to the fact that
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
,
\mathbf{U}
, and
\mathbf{O}_{1}
have orthogonal columns, and the last equality holds by the definition of
\sin\angle
defined in (
C.3
).
Therefore, substituting the error bound of
\|\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U})\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}
in (
C.2
) into (
C.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\|_{\rm{F}}=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\Bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+12}{4}}}{{\textrm{\tt Eigen-gap}(k)}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\Bigg).

\square

C.2
Supporting Lemmas for Theorem
3

Recall that Lemma
2
and Lemma Lemma
3
are key results of our development. We provide their proofs in Appendices
C.2.1
and
C.2.2
respectively. Some additional lemmas that support the proof of Theorem
3
are stated and proved in Appendix
C.2.3
.

C.2.1
Proof of Lemma
2

Proof.

For notational simplicity, let us define

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}\left[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]^{\top},

(C.6)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}}\left[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]^{\top},\textrm{ and }

\displaystyle\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}\left[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}.

The proof is based on the following error decomposition.

Error Decomposition.

We decompose the target operator norm as

\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\underbrace{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}}_{(A)}+\underbrace{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}}_{(B)}+\underbrace{\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}-\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}}_{(C)}+\underbrace{\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}-\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}}_{(D)},

(C.7)

where term
(A)
is the early-stopping error, term
(B)
is the approximation error of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\textrm{NN}}
term
(C)
is the mixing error of forward process (
1
), term
(D)
is the finite-sample error.

Bounding Term
(A)
.

Using the fact that
\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}=e^{-t_{0}/2}\mathbf{R}_{0}+\mathbf{B}_{1-e^{-t_{0}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-e^{-t_{0}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-(1-e^{-t_{0}})\mathbf{I}_{d}=(1-e^{-t_{0}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-\mathbf{I}_{d}).

Therefore, by the definition of
(A)
in (
C.7
) and
t_{0}=n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(A)={\mathcal{O}}\Big(\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})\cdot n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\Big).

(C.8)

Bounding Term
(B)
.

Under the coupled SDE system (
28
), we have

\displaystyle(B)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}\left[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}\left[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}^{\top}\right]\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim P_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}}[\mathbf{R}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})(\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})^{\top}\right]\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

(C.9)

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]^{\top}-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]^{\top}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}},

(C.10)

where the last equality invokes
\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}
defined in (
28
). For term (
C.9
), we have

(
C.9
)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})(\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})^{\top}\right]\right\|_{\text{op}}+\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})(\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow})^{\top}\right]\right\|_{\text{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\right),

(C.11)

where (
C.11
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and rearranging terms. Similarly, for term (
C.10
), using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

(
C.10
)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}])\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]^{\top}\right\|_{\textrm{op}}+\left\|\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}](\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\right]^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}]^{\top})\right\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\right),

(C.12)

Then, substituting (
C.11
) and (
C.12
) into (
C.9
) and (
C.10
), we deduce that

\displaystyle(B)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg((1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\bigg),

(C.13)

where the last equality follows from applying the upper bound (
29
) in Lemma
3
and using the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big\|e^{-t_{0}/2}\mathbf{R}_{0}+\mathbf{B}_{1-e^{-t_{0}}}\big\|_{2}^{2}\leq e^{-t_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{0}\big\|_{2}^{2}+1-e^{-t_{0}}={\mathcal{O}}(1)\ \textrm{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\big\|_{2}^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(1).

Bounding Term
(C)
.

Applying Lemma
3
to the estimated backward process starting from
P_{T}
and
{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
, respectively, we obtain

\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}-\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}={\mathcal{O}}\big(2\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}\big)={\mathcal{O}}\bigg((1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\bigg).

(C.14)

Bounding Term
(D)
.

By introducing the estimation error between
\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]
, we have

\displaystyle(D)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left((\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])^{\top}\right)-\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\|\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}(\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])^{\top}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}+\bigg\|(\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]^{\top}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left((\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])^{\top}\right)-\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}\|_{2}\|\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]\|_{2}+\|\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]\|_{2}\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\bigg\|\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left((\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-1/2}(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])^{\top}(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-1/2}-\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}\bigg\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\bigg\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}\|_{2}\|(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])\|_{2}\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\|_{\textrm{op}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])\|_{2}\|(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}]\|_{2}\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\|_{\textrm{op}},

where
(i)
follows from the H
\ddot{\text{o}}
lder inequality and
(ii)
holds due to the covariance normalization using
(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Applying Theorem 3.1.1 and Theorem 4.6.1 of
Vershynin (2018)
to

(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-1/2}(\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{0}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])\ \textrm{ and }\ \frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-1/2}(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])(\mathbf{R}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}_{i}])^{\top}(\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}})^{-1/2},

respectively, we obtain that with probability
1-\delta
, it holds

\displaystyle(D)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\max\bigg\{\frac{\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)}}{\sqrt{m}},\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)}}{\sqrt{m}}\bigg)^{2}\bigg\}\cdot\big\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}\bigg)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})(1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\bigg),

(C.15)

where the last inequality the last inequality invokes the order of
m
in (
27
) and the fact that

\big\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\big\|\widecheck{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}+\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t_{0}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{0}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big\|_{\textrm{op}}.

Summing up the upper bound of
(A)
–
(D)
in (
C.8
) and (
C.13
)–(
C.15
), we obtain the desired result.
\square

C.2.2
Proof of Lemma
3

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we denote
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\cdot):=\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\cdot,T-t\right)
and let
\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}
be the minimal and maximal elements of
\mathbf{c}
in
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, respectively. First, by direct calculation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\Big)^{\top}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}+\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}+2\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})^{\top}(\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}))\right]}_{(*)}.

Consider
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
, equivalent to
\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
defined via transformation, defined as

\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(\mathbf{z},t):=\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{t}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{z},t),

(C.16)

where
\mathbf{V}
,
\mathbf{D}_{t}
, and
\mathbf{g}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
are components of
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
defined in (
18
). Note that the Lipschitz constant of the ReLU network
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
with respect to
\mathbf{z}
is also on the order of
\gamma
defined in (
22
).
Then, for term
(*)
, we have

\displaystyle(*)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})^{\top}(\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t})-\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}))\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{4}+\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}^{1/2}(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}^{1/2}\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}\mathbf{V})^{-1}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}^{1/2}-\mathbf{I})\mathbf{D}_{T-t}^{1/2}(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\bigg(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}-1)\mathbbm{1}\{\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}>1\}}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}+\frac{(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}-1)\mathbbm{1}\{\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}\leq 1\}}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}\bigg)\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2},

where
(i)
holds due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}(\cdot)
is
\gamma_{1}
-Lipschitz;
(ii)
follows from

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}\mathbf{V})^{-1}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{T-t}-\mathbf{I})=\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}-1,

and

\frac{1}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}\leq\|\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{T-t}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}.

Notice that
\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}\leq 1
is equivalent to
t\leq T-2\log\gamma_{1}
. By Gr
\ddot{\text{o}}
nwall’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{0}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T-t_{0}}2\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}{\rm d}t\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T-2\log\gamma_{1}}\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{2(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}\right){\rm d}t+\int_{T-2\log\gamma_{1}}^{T-t_{0}}\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{2(\alpha_{T-t}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{T-t}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}\alpha_{T-t}^{2}}\right){\rm d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{0}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T-t_{0}}2\mathbb{E}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}{\rm d}t\right)

(C.17)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3}{2}(T-t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{2\log\gamma_{1}}\frac{2(\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{w}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w+\int_{2\log\gamma_{1}}^{T}\frac{2(\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{w}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w\right),

(C.18)

where the last equality follows from rearranging the terms and a change of variable
T-t=w
.

Now we claim that

\int_{t_{0}}^{2\log\gamma_{1}}\frac{2(\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{w}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w+\int_{2\log\gamma_{1}}^{T}\frac{2(\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1)}{h_{w}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\max}^{2}\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w\leq 4\gamma_{1}\left(1-\log(\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}+t_{0})\right)-2(T-t_{0}).

(C.19)

To verify this, consider the integral

\displaystyle\int\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w=

\displaystyle C-\dfrac{2\gamma_{1}\arctan(\sqrt{c-1}e^{-w/2})}{\sqrt{c-1}}-\log(e^{w}+c-1),

\forall\ c>1

(C.20)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w=

\displaystyle C-2\gamma_{1}e^{-w/2}-w,

c=1

(C.21)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w=

\displaystyle C-\dfrac{\gamma_{1}\log\left(\frac{1+e^{-w/2}\sqrt{1-c}}{1-e^{-w/2}\sqrt{1-c}}\right)}{\sqrt{1-c}}-\log(e^{w}+c-1),

\forall\ 0<c<1

(C.22)

1.

For the case
c>1
, note that

\displaystyle

\displaystyle-\log\bigg(\frac{e^{T}+c-1}{e^{t_{0}}+c-1}\bigg)\leq-(T-t_{0})+\log(1+(c-1)e^{-t_{0}})\leq\log(c-(c-1)t_{0})-(T-t_{0}),

(C.23)

\displaystyle-\frac{\arctan(\sqrt{c-1}e^{-T/2})-\arctan(\sqrt{c-1}e^{-t_{0}/2})}{\sqrt{c-1}}\leq e^{-t_{0}/2}-e^{-T/2}\leq 1.

Then, by substituting (
C.23
) into the integral (
C.20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w\leq 2\gamma_{1}+\log(c-(c-1)t_{0})-(T-t_{0}).

(C.24)

2.

For the case
c=1
, applying
e^{-w/2}\leq 1
, we obtain that the integral in (
C.21
) satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w\leq-2\gamma_{1}(e^{-T}-e^{-t_{0}})-(T-t_{0})\leq 2\gamma_{1}-(T-t_{0}).

(C.25)

3.

For the case
0<c<1
, due to the continuity of the integral (
C.22
) with respect to
c
and the bound in (
C.25
), we only need to focus on the case
c\ll 1
. Without loss of generality, we consider
c<1/2
. By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-c}}\left(\log\bigg(\frac{1+e^{-T/2}\sqrt{1-c}}{1-e^{-T/2}\sqrt{1-c}}\bigg)-\log\bigg(\frac{1+e^{-t_{0}/2}\sqrt{1-c}}{1-e^{-t_{0}/2}\sqrt{1-c}}\bigg)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-c}}\log\bigg(\frac{1+e^{-t_{0}/2}\sqrt{1-c}}{1-e^{-t_{0}/2}\sqrt{1-c}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}(\log 4-\log(c+t_{0})),

(C.26)

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
e^{-x}\leq 1/(1+x)
,
\sqrt{1-c}\leq 1-c/2
, and
\log((1+x)/(1-x))
is increasing in
x
; and rearranging terms. Then, by substituting (
C.23
) and (
C.26
) into (
C.22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{\alpha_{w}\gamma_{1}-1}{h_{w}+c\alpha_{w}^{2}}{\rm d}w\leq 2\gamma_{1}(1-\log(c+t_{0}))-(T-t_{0}).

(C.27)

Combining the results in (
C.24
), (
C.25
), and (
C.27
), we verified the claim in (
C.19
).
Finally, applying the upper bound of score estimation in (
29
) and substituting (
C.19
) into (
C.17
) and (
C.18
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{0}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon(T-t_{0})\right)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3}{2}(T-t_{0})+4\gamma_{1}(1-\log(\widehat{\sigma}_{\min}^{2}+t_{0}))-2(T-t_{0})\right)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg((1+\sigma_{\max}^{k})d^{\frac{5}{4}}k^{\frac{k+10}{4}}n^{-\frac{1-\delta(n)}{k+5}}\log^{\frac{5}{2}}n\bigg),

where the last equality follows from invoking
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{R}_{0}^{\leftarrow}-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\|_{2}^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(e^{-T})
and rearranging terms.
\square

C.2.3
Other Supporting Lemmas for Theorem
3

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose that
P_{\mathrm{data}}
is sub-Gaussian, and both
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{r},t)
and
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{r})
are Lipschitz with respect to both
\mathbf{r}
and
t
. Consider the score estimation error satisfying

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{t}\sim P_{t}}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{R}_{t},t)-\nabla\log p_{t}\left(\mathbf{R}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}{\rm d}t={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(T-t_{0})).

Then, the total variation distance is bounded by

\operatorname{TV}(P_{\mathrm{data}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(dt_{0}L_{s}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{2})+\sqrt{\epsilon(T-t_{0})}+\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(P_{\mathrm{data}}\|\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\right)}\exp(-T)\bigg),

(C.28)

where
P_{\mathrm{data}}
is the initial distribution of
\mathbf{R}
in (
8
) and
\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}
is the marginal distribution of the backward process
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}
in (
4
) starting from
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

To estimate
\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})
, we leverage the error decomposition in (
25
).
Recall that
\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}
is the marginal distribution of
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}
in (
4
) initialized with
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim P_{T}
. In the decomposition (
25
),
\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t_{0}})
is the early stopping error,
\operatorname{TV}(P_{t_{0}},\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})
is the statistical error arising from the score estimation, and
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})
is the mixing error of the forward process (
1
).

1.

For term
\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t_{0}})
, applying the upper bound (
C.34
) with
t=t_{0}
in Lemma
C.4
, we obtain

\operatorname{TV}\left(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t_{0}}\right)={\mathcal{O}}(dt_{0}).

(C.29)

2.

For term
\operatorname{TV}(P_{t_{0}},\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})
, by Pinsker’s inequality
(
Tsybakov 2009
, Lemma 2.5)
and the upper bound of KL-divergence (
C.33
) in Lemma
C.3
, we have

\operatorname{TV}(P_{t_{0}},\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})\leq\operatorname{KL}(P_{t_{0}}||\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})={\mathcal{O}}\big(\sqrt{\epsilon\left(T-t_{0}\right)}\big).

(C.30)

3.

For term
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})
, by Pinsker’s inequality
(
Tsybakov 2009
, Lemma 2.5)
and Data processing inequality
(
Thomas and Joy 2006
, Theorem 2.8.1)
, we deduce that

{\operatorname{TV}}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}(\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}\|\widehat{P}_{t_{0}})}\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(P_{T}\|\mathcal{N}\left(0,\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\right)}={\mathcal{O}}\big(\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(P_{\textrm{data}}\|\mathcal{N}\left(0,\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\right)}\exp(-T)\big),

(C.31)

where in the last inequality, we use the exponential mixing property of the O-U process.

Substituting the upper bounds (
C.29
), (
C.30
), and (
C.31
) into (
25
), we obtain the desired result.
\square

Lemma C.2

(Novikov’s condition)
.

Under the assumptions in Lemma
C.1
, it holds

\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t})_{t\in[0,T-t_{0}]}}\bigg[\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T-t_{0}}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t},t)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}{\rm d}t\right)\bigg]<\infty,

(C.32)

where the expectation is taken over the backward diffusion process
(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t})_{t\in[0,T-t_{0}]}
in (
3
).

Proof of Lemma
C.2
.

The result follows from a straightforward calculation using the same techniques as in
(
Chen et al. 2023a
, Lemma 11)
.
\square

Lemma C.3
.

Suppose that the assumptions in Lemma
C.1
hold. When both the ground-truth and the learned backward processes start with
\mathbf{R}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{d}}}{{=}}\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim P_{T}
, the KL-divergence between the laws of the terminal distributions of the processes
\mathbf{R}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{T-t_{0}}^{\leftarrow}
can be bounded by

\operatorname{KL}(P_{t_{0}}||\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}})\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T-t_{0}}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t},T-t\right)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\leftarrow}_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}{\rm d}t\right)={\mathcal{O}}\Big(\epsilon(T-t_{0})\Big).

(C.33)

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

By Lemma
C.2
, the Novikov’s condition holds. By the data processing inequality of the KL divergence
(
Thomas and Joy 2006
, Theorem 2.8.1)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}\big(P_{t_{0}}\big\|\widetilde{P}_{t_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{KL}\big(({\bf R}_{t}^{\leftarrow})_{0\leq t\leq T-t_{0}}||(\widehat{\bf R}_{t}^{\leftarrow})_{0\leq t\leq T-t_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T-t_{0}}\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{R}_{t}^{\leftarrow},T-t)-\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{R}_{t}^{\leftarrow})\big\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right].

Immediately, we obtain the results directly using Girsanov’s Theorem
(
Chen et al. 2023b
, Theorem 9)
.
\square

Lemma C.4
.

Suppose that the assumptions in Lemma
C.1
hold. Then, for any
t<1/d
, we have

\operatorname{TV}(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t})={\mathcal{O}}(dt).

(C.34)

Proof of Lemma
C.4
.

Given
\mathbf{R}_{0}
,
\mathbf{R}_{t}
can be represented as

\mathbf{R}_{t}=e^{-t/2}\mathbf{R}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)/2}{\rm d}\mathbf{W}_{s},

where
\mathbf{R}_{0}
and
\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)/2}{\rm d}\mathbf{W}_{s}
are independent. Then, the density of
\mathbf{R}_{t}
is given by

p_{t}(\mathbf{r})=\int p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{y})\phi(\mathbf{r};\alpha_{t}\mathbf{y},h_{t}){\rm d}\mathbf{y}.

Define

S(d,t):={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d+\log(1/t)})

(C.35)

as a truncation radius and we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{TV}\left(P_{\textrm{data}},P_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\left|p_{t}(\mathbf{r})-p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\right|{\rm d}\mathbf{r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}>S(d,t)}\Big(p_{t}(\mathbf{r})+p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\Big){\rm d}\mathbf{r}

(C.36)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)}\bigg|\int(p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{y})\phi(\mathbf{r};\alpha_{t}\mathbf{y},h_{t})-p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})){\rm d}\mathbf{y}\bigg|{\rm d}\mathbf{r}.

(C.37)

By the density upper bound in (
B.18
) and Theorem 3.1 of
Chazottes, Collet, and Redig (2021)
, it holds that
p_{\textrm{data}}
and
p_{t}(\mathbf{r})
are sub-Gaussian and there exists a constant
A_{1}>0
such that
(p_{t}(\mathbf{r})+p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}))\leq\exp(-A_{1}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}/2)
.

For term (
C.36
), using the sub-Gaussian tail in Proposition 2.6.6 of
Vershynin (2018)
and invoking the order of
S(d,t)
in (
C.35
), we obtain that

\eqref{equ: term1 in TV between 0 and t0}={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{2^{-\frac{d}{2}}dS(d,t)^{d-2}}{A_{1}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}\exp\left(-\frac{A_{1}S(d,t)^{2}}{2}\right)\bigg)={\mathcal{O}}\big(t\exp\left(-A_{1}d\right)\big).

(C.38)

For term (
C.37
), by taking a change of variable
\mathbf{z}:=(\mathbf{r}-\alpha_{t}\mathbf{y})/\sqrt{h_{t}}
, we deduce that

(
C.37
)

\displaystyle=\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)}\left|\int\left(p_{\textrm{data}}(\alpha_{t}^{-1}(\mathbf{r}-\sqrt{h_{t}}\mathbf{z}))\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})-p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\right){\rm d}\mathbf{z}\right|{\rm d}\mathbf{r}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)}\bigg|\int\bigg(\nabla p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}\bigg|{\rm d}\mathbf{r}

(C.39)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)}\bigg|\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)^{\top}\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)\bigg)\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}\bigg|{\rm d}\mathbf{r}\bigg),

(C.40)

where
(i)
involves the Taylor expansion
\alpha_{t}^{-1}=1+t/2+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{2})
,
h_{t}=t+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{2})
and the fact that

p_{\textrm{data}}(e^{t/2}(\mathbf{r}-\sqrt{h_{t}}\mathbf{z}))=p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)^{\top}\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg).

The integrals associated with the kernel function
\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
satisfy

\int p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}=p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})

(C.41)

and

\displaystyle\int\nabla p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}=\frac{t}{2}\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\mathbf{r}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})={\mathcal{O}}(t\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\|\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\|_{2}\cdot p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})).

(C.42)

Moreover, since the Hessian matrix satisfies the following property

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})=(\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top})\cdot p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}),

(C.43)

we deduce

\displaystyle\quad\int\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)^{\top}\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg(\frac{t\mathbf{r}}{2}-\sqrt{t}\mathbf{z}\bigg)\bigg)\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\bigg(\frac{1}{8}t^{2}\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\mathbf{r}\mathbf{r}^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}t\nabla^{2}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{8}t^{2}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{r}^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}t\bigg)(\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top})\cdot p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\bigg)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg((t^{2}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}+t)\operatorname{tr}\big((\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top})\cdot p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\big)\bigg).

(C.44)

where
(i)
is follows from
\int\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{\top}\phi(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}){\rm d}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{I}_{d}
and rearranging terms, and
(ii)
follows (
C.43
).

Therefore, by substituting (
C.41
), (
C.42
) and (
C.44
) into (
C.39
) and (
C.40
), we obtain that

(
C.37
)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\int_{\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)}\bigg(t\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\|\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\operatorname{tr}\big((t^{2}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}+t)(\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top})\big)\bigg)p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}){\rm d}\mathbf{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(tS(d,t)\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{0}\sim P_{\textrm{data}}}[\|\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{R}_{0})\|_{2}^{2}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+(t^{2}S^{2}(d,t)+t)\operatorname{tr}\bigg(\int(\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})+\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top})p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}){\rm d}\mathbf{r})\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}{\mathcal{O}}\big(t\sqrt{d}S(d,t)+t^{2}dS^{2}(d,t)\cdot L_{s}(\sigma_{\max}^{2}+1)\big)={\mathcal{O}}\big(dtL_{s}(\sigma_{\max}^{2}+1)\big).

(C.45)

where
(i)
is due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\leq S(d,t)
, and
(ii)
invokes the upper bound (
C.46
) in Lemma
C.5
.

Combining the upper bound of (
C.36
) and (
C.37
) in (
C.38
) and (
C.45
), we obtain the desired result.
\square

Lemma C.5
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
-
3
holds. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{0}\sim P_{\mathrm{data}}}\left\|\nabla\log p_{\mathrm{data}}(\mathbf{R}_{0})\right\|_{2}^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(dL_{s}(\sigma_{\max}^{2}+1)).

(C.46)

Proof of Lemma
C.5
.

Taking
t=0
in the formula of
\nabla\log p_{t}
in (
12
) of Lemma
1
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{\textrm{sub}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{0}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{r},0)-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbf{I}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{0}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{r}.

Under Assumption
3
, for any
\mathbf{r}_{1},\mathbf{r}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}_{1})-\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}_{2})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{s}\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{0}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1}\|_{\textrm{op}}\|\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L_{s}(\sigma_{\max}^{2}+1)}{\sigma_{d}^{2}}\cdot\|\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}\|_{2},

(C.47)

where the last equality follows from
\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{0}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{0}^{-1}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq 1/\sigma_{d}^{2}
. This indicates that the Lipschitz constant of
\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}
is bounded by
L_{s}(1+\sigma_{\max}^{2})/\sigma_{d}^{2}
.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_{0}\sim P_{\textrm{data}}}\bigg[\|\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{R}_{0})\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}\bigg(\int\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})\nabla\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}){\rm d}\mathbf{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\bigg(-\int\nabla^{2}\log p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r})^{\top}p_{\textrm{data}}(\mathbf{r}){\rm d}\mathbf{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(dL_{s}(\sigma_{\max}^{2}+1)),

where
(i)
is due to the integration by parts and the last inequality follows from invoking (
C.47
).
\square

Appendix D
Additional Details of the Numerical Study with Synthetic Data

Here we explain additional details of the numerical experiment setup for Section
6
. Following the standard setup in the econometrics literature
(
Bai and Ng 2002
,
Bai and Ng 2023
)
, we construct the ground-truth environment of high-dimensional asset returns using a sub-Gaussian factor model. Specifically, the universe consists of
d=2048
assets, whose returns are driven by
k=16
latent factors. Here, the choice of
d
as a power of 2 enhances the computational efficiency.

Denote
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F}=(\mu_{F1},\mu_{F2},\dots,\mu_{Fk})
as the expected return and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{F}=\operatorname{diag}\{\sigma_{F1}^{2},\sigma_{F2}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{Fk}^{2}\}
the covariance matrix of the latent factor. In addition, denote

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}=\operatorname{diag}\{\sigma_{\varepsilon 1}^{2},\sigma_{\varepsilon 2}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{\varepsilon d}^{2}\}
as the covariance of the idiosyncratic noise of the asset. We then construct samples from the ground-truth environment as follows:

1.

Latent Factor.

The components of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F}
are drawn i.i.d. from
\operatorname{Uniform}(0,0.1)
and we set
\sigma_{Fi}=1.5\mu_{Fi}
for
i=1,2,\dots,k
to ensure that the volatility scales proportionally to the corresponding mean.

2.

Factor Loadings.

We generate the factor loading matrix
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
, where each element is drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, ensuring that the loadings are symmetrically distributed with comparable magnitudes across assets and factors.

3.

Idiosyncratic Risk.

\{\sigma_{\varepsilon i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
are drawn i.i.d. from
\operatorname{Uniform}(0,0.4)
, ensuring uncorrelated idiosyncratic returns across assets.

4.

Asset Return.
We generate a total of
2^{13}=8192
simulated samples. Asset returns are sampled i.i.d. according to the following procedure. First, the factor is drawn from a multivariate normal distribution
\mathbf{F}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{F})
. Then the asset-specific noise terms are drawn i.i.d. from
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})
. Finally, the asset return is constructed by
\mathbf{R}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathbf{F}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
. We denote by
\mu_{Ri}
and
\sigma_{Ri}
the mean and standard deviation of the ground-truth return for asset
i
, where
i=1,2,\dots,d
.

Summary Statistics of the Synthetic Data.

To show that our simulation setting is close to the realistic market scenario, we benchmark our simulation set-up against the S&P 500 index. Specifically, denote by
\mu_{\text{S\&P 500},i}
and
\sigma_{\text{S\&P500},i}
the mean and standard deviation of historical returns for stock
i
in the S&P 500 index over the period 2000–2020. Table
D.1
reports the summary statistics of
\{\mu_{Ri}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and compares them with
\{\mu_{\text{S\&P500},i}\}_{i=1}^{500}
. The range of both the simulated mean and standard deviation of returns closely matches that of the empirical quantities of stocks in the S&P 500 index.

In addition, the variance of the factors accounts for 50.42% of the total variance in our synthetic data, which corresponds to the population
R
-squared.

Summary statistics for simulation return data and comparison with S&P 500 over the period 2000-2020.

Data Preprocessing.

We preprocess the data in the following steps.

1.

First, we sort the asset returns by their variance, prioritizing those with greater variability for subsequent analysis.

2.

Next, we normalize the data by subtracting the mean return of each asset and reshape the data from a one-dimensional vector of length
2^{11}
into a two-dimensional matrix of size
(2^{5},2^{6})
. This reshaping step ensures compatibility with the 2D-Unet architecture and allows the model to effectively leverage spatial hierarchies in the data.

Training.

We train our diffusion factor model using a 2-dimensional U-Net architecture
(
Ronneberger, Fischer, and Brox 2015
)
, which is a convolutional encoder-decoder network with skip connections. The U-Net serves as a practical implementation of the encoder-decoder architecture analyzed in theory. Specifically, in our theoretical analysis, we consider a linear encoder-decoder architecture, which first projects the input data into a low-dimensional latent space and then reconstructs it back to the original space. This
project-then-lift network architecture
not only reduces the number of trainable parameters by operating in a low-dimensional latent space, but also effectively captures data intrinsic structures such as the factor model in Assumption
1
. In experiments, U-Net implements the same encoder-decoder architecture with nonlinear networks, which is further shown to accommodate complex real-world data, such as the U.S. stock returns in Section
7
and Appendix
E
. Nonetheless, whether the mappings are linear or nonlinear is
secondary
, since linear transformations are only assumed to enable tractable analysis and are standard in the theoretical study of deep learning
(
Baldi and Hornik 1989
,
Chen et al. 2012
,
Chen et al. 2023a
,
Weitzner et al. 2025
)
.

Besides, guided by the theoretical results in
Sections 3-5, we choose the latent space dimension—the bottleneck dimension of the encoder and decoder architecture—based on the underlying factor model. More specifically, in synthetic experiments, we set the bottleneck width equal to the true factor dimension,
k=16
. The U-Net has approximately one billion parameters and is trained to approximate the score function by minimizing the empirical loss defined in (
7
). To assess performance under different data regimes, we set the number of training samples to be
N=2^{9},2^{10},\dots,2^{13}
. For fairness, both
Diff Method
and
Emp Method
are trained on the
same

N
simulated returns for each
N
. The
Diff Method
then uses the trained model to generate
2^{13}
new samples for latent subspace recovery and distribution estimation, while the
Emp Method
provides empirical estimates solely on
N
training samples. To ensure that the results are robust rather than due to chance, each experiment is repeated five times.

Appendix E
Additional Details of the Empirical Analysis

In this section, we provide further details on the empirical setup in Section
7
and report robustness analysis with respect to transaction costs, risk aversions, norm constraints, and model update frequency.

E.1
Data Preprocessing, Training, and Evaluation

Data Selection and Preprocessing.

We select and preprocess the stock return data in the following steps:

1.

We first exclude stocks with more than 5% missing values and then select the 512 stocks with the largest market capitalizations from the remaining universe.

2.

Rank the selected stocks by return volatility in descending order.

3.

Within each rolling window of the training data, we standardize the returns by subtracting the (empirical) mean and dividing by the (empirical) standard deviation for each stock.

4.

Winsorize returns for each stock at 2.5% each side by resampling non-extreme values with the same sign, which preserves the empirical distribution while mitigating the influence of outliers
(
Tukey 1962
)
.

Training and Sampling.

We employ a 2D-UNet architecture with approximately one billion parameters to train our diffusion factor model. For real data, the true factor dimension is unknown; following common practice, we set the bottleneck width to
8
as an educated estimate consistent with factor dimensions commonly used in the literature
(
Fama and French 1993
,
Bai and Ng 2002
,
Onatski 2010
,
Fama and French 2015b
,
Fan, Guo, and Zheng 2022
,
Bai and Ng 2023
)
. Following a similar setup as
Lyu et al. (2022)
, we set the total number of training steps to
T=200
and apply early stopping at
T^{\prime}=180
for the sampling of time-reversed process (
4
). For the downstream evaluation on mean-variance portfolios and factor-tangency portfolios, we use the trained model to generate
2^{12}=4096
new samples for each rolling window.

Performance Evaluations.

Next, we specify the performance evaluation metrics used in Section
7
.

1.

SR is defined as
\hat{\mu}/\hat{\sigma}
, where
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\sigma}
denote the sample mean and standard deviation, respectively, of excess portfolio returns over the testing periods.

2.

CER is defined as
\hat{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta\hat{\sigma}^{2}
, where
\eta
is the risk aversion parameter.

3.

MDD is defined as

\text{MDD}=\max_{t\in\mathcal{D}_{t}}\left(\frac{\max_{s\leq t}V_{s}-V_{t}}{\max_{s\leq t}V_{s}}\right),

where
\mathcal{D}_{t}
contains all the dates of the test set and
V_{t}
denotes the portfolio value on day
t
.

4.

TO on day
t
is defined as

\displaystyle\textrm{TO}_{t}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\left|w_{i,t}-\frac{w_{i,t-1}(1+r_{i,t-1})}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i,t-1}(1+r_{i,t-1})}\right|,

where
\mathcal{A}_{t}
contains all assets of the test set on day
t
,
w_{i,t}
is the target weight of stock
i
on day
t
, and
r_{i,t}
denotes the return of stock
i
on day
t
.

We visualize the return distribution generated by our diffusion factor model for selected assets in Figure
E.1
(trained on data from May 1, 2009 to April 30, 2014), which is compared with the observed training data. The generated data distribution is smoother and closely approximates the empirical distribution.

Figure E.1:
Examples of asset return distribution (the blue histogram is constructed using samples generated from the diffusion model and the green one uses actual data samples.)

(a)

The asset with the largest variance.

(b)

The asset with the smallest variance.

(c)

The asset with the largest mean.

(d)

The asset with the smallest mean.

\eta=3
, we report out-of-sample portfolio performance under the scenario without transaction costs in Table
E.1
. The
Diff Emp+Diff Emp
outperforms all other methods, which are consistent with those observed in the scenario with transaction costs.

E.3
Robustness Analysis on Norm Constraints

As a robustness check on the choice of norm constraints, for mean-variance portfolios, we solve the target weight by replacing the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm constraint in (
34
) with an
\ell_{1}
-norm constraint
\|\boldsymbol{\omega}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|w_{i}|\leq 3
.
We report the out-of-sample portfolio performance with and without transaction costs in Table
E.3
and plot the cumulative returns with transaction costs (in log scale) in Figure
E.3
. The
Diff Emp+Diff Emp
achieves the highest Mean, SR, and CER, which is similar to the results under the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm constraint.

Figure E.3:
Cumulative returns of different portfolios in log scale with transaction cost under
\ell_{1}
norm constraints.

E.4
Robustness Analysis on Update Frequency

As a robustness check on the model update frequency, we also evaluate an annual update scheme with a rolling five-year window. Specifically, on May 1 of each year
T
, we reselect and pre-process the stocks, and update the model parameters using training data from May 1 of year
T-5
to April 30 of year
T
. We test the model on data from May 1 of year
T
to April 30 of year
T+1
to evaluate out-of-sample performance.

For mean-variance portfolios, we report out-of-sample portfolio performance under scenarios without and with transaction costs in Tables
E.4
and
E.5
for
\eta=3
and
\eta=5
, respectively. Additionally, we plot the cumulative returns with transaction cost (in log scale) for
\eta=3
and
\eta=5
in Figure
E.4
.
Diff Emp+Diff Emp
outperforms all alternatives, which is consistent with the result observed under quarterly updates.

\eta=3

Moreover, for factor tangency portfolios, we report Sharpe ratios across varying number of factors in Table
E.6
and plot the correlation heatmaps between top eight factors estimated using diffusion-based methods and traditional factors in

in Figure

. The diffusion-based methods exhibit higher Sharpe ratios and outperform all other approaches.
